    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-foundation-cohomology",
        "exact_quote": "Over a Noetherian base, the higher direct images of coherent sheaves under a proper morphism are coherent. Over an affine base their cohomology commutes with flat affine base change. For a projective scheme over an affine Noetherian base and a relatively ample line bundle, sufficiently high powers give projective embeddings, and sufficiently high twists annihilate higher coherent cohomology. The ample section ring is finitely generated and its $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Proj}}$ recovers the scheme. Finite pullback preserves ampleness; tensor products of relatively ample line bundles are relatively ample, and a line bundle is relatively ample if a positive power is. Formation of $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Proj}}$ commutes with base change. On a projective curve over a field, coherent cohomology vanishes in degrees greater than one, and for sheaves of finite support in degrees greater than zero. Global functions on a geometrically integral projective variety over a field equal the ground field. For proper coherence, flat cohomology base change, and ample twists, respectively, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 02O5, 02KH and 0B5T) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. For Proj recovery and the ampleness rules, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 0EKI, 0892, 0890 and 02NN) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].",
        "source_id": "PM-202.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

          "exact_quote": "Over a Noetherian base, the higher direct images of coherent sheaves under a proper morphism are coherent. The ample section ring is finitely generated and its $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Proj}}$ recovers the scheme.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-foundation-duality",
        "exact_quote": "We use the transitivity sequence $$\\Omega_{V/U}\\otimes_VW\\longrightarrow\\Omega_{W/U}\n \\longrightarrow\\Omega_{W/V}\\longrightarrow0$$ and the conormal sequence for a closed immersion. Let $S$ be a field. On $\\mathbb P^b_S$, $\\det\\Omega_{\\mathbb P^b_S/S}=\\mathcal O(-b-1)$. For a coherent sheaf $\\mathcal G$ there, Serre duality identifies $$H^i(\\mathcal G)^\\vee\n \\simeq\\operatorname{Ext}^{b-i}\n       (\\mathcal G,\\mathcal O(-b-1)).$$ We use the local-to-global Ext spectral sequence and the Koszul resolution of a quotient by a regular sequence. These inputs will yield the required absolute determinant-degree formula even when the curve is not smooth over $S$. For the projective-space dualizing complex and the adjunction formalism, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0A9W and Section 48.15) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. The local-to-global Ext sequence is (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0BQP) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].",
        "source_id": "PM-202.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-foundation-duality",
          "exact_quote": "We use the transitivity sequence $$\\Omega_{V/U}\\otimes_VW\\longrightarrow\\Omega_{W/U}\n \\longrightarrow\\Omega_{W/V}\\longrightarrow0$$ and the conormal sequence for a closed immersion. For a coherent sheaf $\\mathcal G$ there, Serre duality identifies $$H^i(\\mathcal G)^\\vee\n \\simeq\\operatorname{Ext}^{b-i}\n       (\\mathcal G,\\mathcal O(-b-1)).$$ We use the local-to-global Ext spectral sequence and the Koszul resolution of a quotient by a regular sequence. These inputs will yield the required absolute determinant-degree formula even when the curve is not smooth over $S$. For the projective-space dualizing complex and the adjunction formalism, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0A9W and Section 48.15) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. The local-to-global Ext sequence is (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0BQP) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-foundation-elliptic",
        "exact_quote": "A smooth Weierstrass cubic with its point at infinity is an elliptic curve. A generalized Weierstrass equation over a discrete valuation ring with unit discriminant gives a smooth elliptic scheme, polarized by three times its origin. Its relative cotangent line is trivial with invariant differential $\\eta$, and multiplication by an integer $a$ pulls $\\eta$ back to $a\\eta$. For a positive integer $a$, multiplication by $a$ on an elliptic curve or elliptic scheme is finite locally free of degree $a^2$. For the relative group law, see (Deligne and Rapoport 1973, II, Proposition 2.7) [[DeligneRapoport1973](#ref-DeligneRapoport1973)]; the unit-discriminant Weierstrass family and the invariant cotangent description are given in (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 072S and 047I) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. See also (Silverman 2009, vol. 106, chap. III) [[Silverman2009](#ref-Silverman2009)] for the Weierstrass formulation. Multiplication is finite flat and surjective by (Milne 2022, Proposition 20.7) [[MilneAV2022](#ref-MilneAV2022)]; over the Noetherian bases here it is finite locally free by (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 02KB) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].",

          "anchor": "stmt-foundation-elliptic",
          "exact_quote": "A smooth Weierstrass cubic with its point at infinity is an elliptic curve. A generalized Weierstrass equation over a discrete valuation ring with unit discriminant gives a smooth elliptic scheme, polarized by three times its origin. Its relative cotangent line is trivial with invariant differential $\\eta$, and multiplication by an integer $a$ pulls $\\eta$ back to $a\\eta$. For a positive integer $a$, multiplication by $a$ on an elliptic curve or elliptic scheme is finite locally free of degree $a^2$. For the relative group law, see (Deligne and Rapoport 1973, II, Proposition 2.7) [[DeligneRapoport1973](#ref-DeligneRapoport1973)]; the unit-discriminant Weierstrass family and the invariant cotangent description are given in (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 072S and 047I) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. See also (Silverman 2009, vol. 106, chap. III) [[Silverman2009](#ref-Silverman2009)] for the Weierstrass formulation.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-foundation-complex",
        "exact_quote": "Finite étale algebraic covers of a smooth complex algebraic curve correspond to finite unramified topological covers of its analytification, including morphisms. For a punctured projective curve, normalization of the original projective curve in the resulting algebraic function field gives the finite compact cover. Normal complex curves are smooth, and the local punctured covering completes by a power map, so monodromy cycle lengths give ramification indices. Topological covers are classified by fundamental-group actions on finite fibers. The fundamental group of a genus-one surface punctured at $n$ points has generators $x,y,u_1,\\ldots,u_n$ with the single relation $$[x,y]u_1\\cdots u_n=1.$$ For a finite map of smooth proper curves over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, Riemann–Hurwitz states $$2g(C)-2=\\deg(f)\\bigl(2g(C')-2\\bigr)\n              +\\sum_{P\\in C}(e_P-1).$$ For the algebraic/topological cover correspondence, see (Milne 2008, Theorem 3.4) [[MilneLEC2008](#ref-MilneLEC2008)]; for Riemann–Hurwitz, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, sec. 53.12, Tag 0C1B) [[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].",
        "source_id": "PM-202.1"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **chain of p-subgroups** ([source](#stmt-def-p-chains)): A chain in $X$ is a strictly increasing sequence of $p$-subgroups $$\mathcal C=(1=Q_0<Q_1<\cdots<Q_r), \qquad \|\mathcal C\|=r.$$ The length-zero chain $(1)$ is included. Set $$H_{\mathcal C}=\bigcap_{i=0}^rN_X(Q_i), \qquad E_{\mathcal C}=\operatorname{Br}_{Q_r}(E).$$ Call $\mathcal C$ *normal* if every $Q_i$ is normal in $Q_r$.
- **normal chain** ([source](#stmt-def-p-chains)): Call $\mathcal C$ *normal* if every $Q_i$ is normal in $Q_r$.
- **p-weight** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A *$p$-weight* of $G$ is a pair $(Q,\phi)$, where $Q\leq G$ is a possibly trivial $p$-subgroup and $$\phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(N_G(Q)/Q),\qquad \phi(1)_p=\|N_G(Q)/Q\|_p.$$
- **block assignment convention** ([source](#eq:block-assignment)): We say that $b^G=B$ when
- **set of weight classes of a block** ([source](#eq:block-assignment)): Let $W_p(B)$ be the set of simultaneous $G$-conjugacy classes of weights assigned to $B$.
- **coefficient restriction** ([source](#eq:block-assignment)): For $H\leq G$, coefficient restriction is the linear map
- **Brauer map** ([source](#pm-h-10)): For a $p$-subgroup $Q$, let $\operatorname{Br}_Q$ denote restriction of coefficients from the $Q$-invariants $(kX)^Q$ to $kC_X(Q)$.
- **section of a group** ([source](#pm-h-11)): A *section* is the set of elements whose $p$-parts are conjugate to a fixed $p$-element.
- **semisimple block** ([source](#pm-h-13)): Call a block *semisimple* if its algebra is semisimple
- **index of a curve** ([source](#pm-h-18)): For a regular projective integral curve over a field, its *index* is the positive generator of the subgroup of $\mathbb Z$ consisting of divisor degrees; equivalently, it is the greatest common divisor of the degrees of its closed points.
- **Euler characteristic and virtual genus invariant** ([source](#pm-h-6)): For such a projective curve $C$, we set $$\chi(C)=\chi(\mathcal O_C),\qquad \nu(C)=-2\chi(C).$$
- **immediate extension** ([source](#pm-h-6)): A field extension is *immediate* if it changes neither the value group nor the residue field.
- **spherically complete field** ([source](#pm-h-26)): A valued field is *spherically complete* if every nested family of nonempty closed balls has nonempty intersection.
- **marked J-cover** ([source](#pm-h-27)): A *marked $J$-cover* of $\mathcal X_{\overline{K_s}}$ will mean a connected smooth projective curve $Y$ with a finite map to $\mathcal X_{\overline{K_s}}$ which is Galois of group $J$, together with a specified left action of $J$ by deck transformations. Isomorphisms are over the fixed base curve and commute with every element of this specified action.
- **number of simple modules of a block** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a block $B$, let $l(B)$ be the number of simple $kG$-modules belonging to $B$, equivalently the number of its irreducible Brauer characters.

| id | quantity | relation | value | regime |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| q-weight-count-equality | l(B) | = | $\|W_p(B)\|$ | Every prime, group and block |
| q-local-global-lower-bound | l(B) | ≥ | $l(b)$ | Any prime and finite group |
| q-abelian-defect-equality | l(B) | = | $l(b)$ | Any prime and finite group |
| q-sylow-normalizer-bound | \|\mathop{\mathrm{IBr}}_p(G)\| | ≥ | $\|\mathop{\mathrm{IBr}}_p(N_G(P))\|$ | Any prime and finite group |
| q-principal-block-bound-three-classes | l(B_0) | ≥ | $(p-1)/2$ | Any prime |
| q-principal-block-bound-general | l(B_0) | ≥ | $2\sqrt{p-1}+1-k_p(G)$ | Any prime dividing the group order |
| q-tuple-threshold | s | ≥ | $s_0$ | Threshold depends on p, J and h but not on the multiset |
| q-genus-rounding-threshold | p^{s-h} | > | $m\bigl(h+\log_p m\bigr)$ | m is the group order |
| q-inseparable-genus-bound | \nu(Z)/d | ≥ | $\sum_{i=1}^n\left(1-\frac1{r_i}\right)$ | Cover degree at most the group order |
| q-euler-degree-formula | \chi(C,\mathcal L) | = | $\deg_S\mathcal L+\chi(C)$ | Degrees over the ground field |
| q-absolute-determinant-degree | \deg_S\det\Omega_{C/\kappa} | = | $\nu(C)$ | No geometric reducedness required |
| q-separable-different-order | \operatorname{ord}_y R_f | ≥ | $a_y-1+\mathbf 1_{p\mid a_y}$ | Residue extension need not be separable |
| q-height-one-genus | \nu(C) | = | $q\nu(C')+(p-1)\deg_S\mathcal F$ | q is the function-field degree |
| q-full-residue-degree | [\operatorname{res}(L):F_0] | = | $[L:K_s]$ | Includes inseparable residue degree |
| q-residue-degree-bound | \sum_{i=1}^r[k_i:k_T] | ≤ | $[U:T]$ | Value group is the rationals |
| q-curve-index | index of the twisted curve | = | $n$ | n is a power of p |
| q-torsion-point-order | order of the point P | = | $p^s$ | Exact order |
| q-label-rank-lower-bound | rank of pulled-back differentials | ≥ | $\|I\|-h$ | Extension of bounded p-part |
| q-small-orbit-degree | [L:K_s]_p | < | $p^h$ | Small Sylow orbit |
| q-residue-degree-sum | sum of residue degrees over the orbit | = | $m$ | m is the group order |
| q-inertia-inseparable-degree | d | = | $\|I_w\|$ | A power of p |
| q-generic-hurwitz | normalized genus invariant of the cover | = | $\sum_{i=1}^n\left(1-\frac1{e_i}\right)$ | Base of genus one |
| q-sharp-specialization-indices | r_i a_i | = | $e_i$ | Each marked point |
| q-nu-definition | \nu(C) | = | $-2\chi(C)$ | Additive on disjoint unions |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-normalizer-projection** [claim](#stmt-lem-normalizer-projection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-normalizer-projection): Orbit cancellation under the p-subgroup action, then invariants of modules of p-groups -> Brauer map is a unital homomorphism and block assignment by central character is well defined
- **s-chain-inversion** [claim](#stmt-lem-chain-transform) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-chain-transform): Identify iterates of the transform with normal chains and invert the finite sum -> Equivalence with an alternating sum over normal chain orbits
- **s-cocenter-ranks** [claim](#stmt-lem-cocenter-ranks) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cocenter-ranks): Frobenius semilinear map on the cocenter and stabilization of images -> Ranks equal simple-module and ordinary-character counts
- **s-subsections** [claim](#stmt-lem-subsections) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-subsections): Section decomposition of the cocenter and a trace pairing -> Subsection count and support preservation
- **s-nonnormal-cancellation** [claim](#stmt-lem-nonnormal-cancellation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-nonnormal-cancellation): Sign-reversing involution inserting or removing a normal closure -> Nonnormal chains cancel
- **s-alternating-subsections** [claim](#stmt-lem-alternating-subsections) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-alternating-subsections): Expand by subsections and toggle a p-group in the chain -> Alternating sums of simple and ordinary counts agree
- **s-defect-zero-detection** [claim](#stmt-lem-defect-zero-detection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-defect-zero-detection): Dual-basis tensor identities, trace argument and Schur scalar of the commutator sum -> Radical annihilation and detection of semisimple blocks
- **s-detecting-element** [claim](#stmt-lem-detecting-element) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-detecting-element): Support and central-character comparison using section preservation -> Central element with zero-or-one residues
- **s-trace-limit** [claim](#stmt-lem-trace-limit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-trace-limit): Valuation estimate for iterated powers of eigenvalues -> Limit of the alternating trace sum
- **s-tuple-trace** [claim](#stmt-lem-tuple-trace) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tuple-trace): Commutator sum scalar on irreducibles and regular trace -> Trace as a weighted tuple count
- **s-pcore-cancellation** [claim](#stmt-lem-pcore-cancellation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pcore-cancellation): Toggle the product of a chain member with the normal p-subgroup -> Vanishing signed chain count when the p-core is nontrivial
- **s-group-reduction** [claim](#stmt-prop-group-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-group-reduction): Rewrite the trace sum over generated subgroups and apply tuple divisibility to its numerator -> Transform identity for all pairs, conditional on tuple divisibility
- **s-ordinary-elliptic** [claim](#stmt-lem-ordinary-elliptic) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ordinary-elliptic): Explicit cubic, point count modulo p by a coefficient calculation, then multiplication surjectivity -> Elliptic curve with a point of exact prime-power order
- **s-cycle-sum** [claim](#stmt-lem-cycle-sum-rationality) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cycle-sum-rationality): Multiplication by p lands in p-th power fields, then Galois descent -> Rational group-law sum of cycles
- **s-constant-product-maps** [claim](#stmt-lem-constant-product-maps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-constant-product-maps): Rigidity of homomorphisms, torsion points and extension over a function field -> Constant plus endomorphisms form of rational maps
- **s-twist-index** [claim](#stmt-prop-twist-index) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-twist-index): Cyclic translated twist, projective Galois descent, comparison of endomorphisms in the descent relation -> Genus-one curve of exact index with marked divisor
- **s-index-base-change** [claim](#stmt-cor-index-base-change) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-index-base-change): Pushforward of divisors and degree comparison over the base -> Divisibility of degrees after bounded-p-part extension
- **s-label-rank** [claim](#stmt-lem-label-rank) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-label-rank): Kernel of the differential map controlled by the inseparable part of the compositum -> Rank bound for marked differentials
- **s-curve-degrees** [claim](#stmt-lem-curve-degrees) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-curve-degrees): Filtration by closed points and free finite modules over discrete valuation rings -> Degree and Euler characteristic formulas
- **s-absolute-genus** [claim](#stmt-prop-absolute-genus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-absolute-genus): Regular immersion, conormal sequence and Serre duality -> Determinant degree equals the genus invariant
- **s-separable-different** [claim](#stmt-lem-separable-different) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-separable-different): Determinant of the pullback of absolute differentials -> Ramification divisor with wild lower bound
- **s-height-one** [claim](#stmt-lem-height-one) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-height-one): Local presentation by p-th power relations and Frobenius transition of cokernel coordinates -> Exact genus relation for exponent-one maps
- **s-inseparable-genus** [claim](#stmt-thm-inseparable-genus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-height-one-2): Frobenius tower, vanishing at ramified labels, weighted sum, then rounding by index divisibility -> Exact inseparable genus bound
- **s-mixed-lift** [claim](#stmt-prop-mixed-lift) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-mixed-lift): Lift the elliptic scheme and torsion section, take the product, complete and descend by étale Galois descent -> Lifted curve, divisor and marked sections
- **s-orthogonal-bases** [claim](#stmt-lem-orthogonal-bases) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orthogonal-bases): Best approximation in a spherically complete field and induction on dimension -> Orthogonal basis and full-dimensional reduction
- **s-simultaneous-residues** [claim](#stmt-lem-simultaneous-residues) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-simultaneous-residues): Approximation elements separating places and independence of lifted residue bases -> Surjective simultaneous residue map and degree bound
- **s-spherical-base** [claim](#stmt-prop-spherical-base) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-spherical-base): Factorial roots, maximal immediate extension, pseudo-limit argument, orthogonal bases -> Spherically complete base with full residue degrees
- **s-marked-cover-classification** [claim](#stmt-lem-marked-cover-classification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-marked-cover-classification): Fundamental group of the punctured torus, monodromy and Riemann existence -> Cover classes as tuples, Galois-stable
- **s-small-orbit-descent** [claim](#stmt-prop-small-orbit-descent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-small-orbit-descent): Central extension by the center, Sylow splitting and Galois descent -> Descended cover over an extension of small p-part
- **s-residue-permutations** [claim](#stmt-lem-residue-permutations) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-residue-permutations): Uniqueness of the valuation and degree sandwich -> Matching permutation actions on marked points
- **s-gauss-section-rule** [claim](#stmt-lem-gauss-section-rule) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-gauss-section-rule): Free module of sections over the base ring and coefficient minimum valuation -> Gauss valuation and surjective reduction of sections
- **s-section-specialization** [claim](#stmt-lem-section-specialization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-section-specialization): Characteristic polynomial of a section over the group and normality -> Injection into sections of residue curves
- **s-inseparable-residue-quotient** [claim](#stmt-lem-inseparable-residue-quotient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-inseparable-residue-quotient): Simultaneous approximation and reduction of the conjugate polynomial -> Purely inseparable quotient of inertia degree
- **s-specialization-model** [claim](#stmt-prop-specialization-model) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-specialization-model): Graded section algebra, Veronese regrading, Nakayama and Proj -> Flat finitely presented model with prescribed fibers
- **s-valuation-model-connectedness** [claim](#stmt-lem-valuation-model-connectedness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-valuation-model-connectedness): Approximation over a normal Noetherian subring and Stein connectedness -> Geometrically connected special fiber
- **s-no-small-cover** [claim](#stmt-prop-no-small-cover) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-valuation-model-connectedness-2): Connected special fiber forces one valuation, whose inertia is a nontrivial normal p-subgroup -> Contradiction with trivial p-core
- **s-tuple-divisibility** [claim](#stmt-thm-tuple-divisibility) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-valuation-model-connectedness-3): Sylow orbits have p-power size and none is small -> Uniform divisibility of tuple counts
- **s-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-valuation-model-connectedness-4): Apply the group reduction to the block pair and read off the weight count -> Block weight equality
- **s-block-consequences** [claim](#stmt-cor-block-consequences) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-valuation-model-connectedness-5): Sum local inequalities over maximal-defect blocks -> Local-global and principal-block bounds
- **s-character-data** [claim](#stmt-cor-character-data) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-character-data): Supply the numerical weight hypothesis to the cited classification -> Classification of defect and Brauer counts
- **s-functorial-awc** [claim](#stmt-cor-functorial-awc) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-functorial-awc): Supply the remaining coefficient from the chain formulation -> Functorial identity in the Grothendieck group

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-orbit-count-from-tuples**: For a finite group J, the number of simultaneous-conjugation orbits of generating tuples equals the number of actual generating tuples multiplied by the order of the centre of J and divided by the order of J, because every generating tuple has the centre of J as its stabiliser. This is the identity linking an exhaustive tuple enumeration to the orbit count N_{J,s}(M) of the tuple-divisibility theorem.
- **inv-tuple-count-regime**: The quantity N_{J,s}(M) is a count of ordered tuples (x,y,u_1,...,u_n) of a finite group J with n=p^s, with the commutator relation, generation of J and prescribed multiset of classes of p-singular entries, taken modulo simultaneous conjugation; the p^h divisibility is asserted for all s at or beyond a threshold depending on p, J and h only, and for every such multiset.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-effective-threshold**: Use the exhaustive counter on small groups to read off a threshold exponent and apply it to other groups or primes. -> The threshold depends on the prime, the group and the divisibility exponent, and is only shown to exist; small-case counts do not give a valid bound for other groups.
- **anti-nontrivial-pcore**: Cite the counter output for a group with a nontrivial normal p-subgroup as evidence for the divisibility theorem. -> The divisibility theorem is only asserted when the largest normal p-subgroup is trivial; counts for other groups carry no such guarantee.
- **anti-infinite-group**: Feed an infinite or continuous group, or a group not given by finitely many permutations, to the enumeration. -> Sylow subgroups, p-singular elements and finite tuple sets are not available, so the enumeration is undefined or non-terminating.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `algorithmic`. For a fixed finite permutation group J, prime p and exponent s, the number N_{J,s}(M) of generating tuples modulo simultaneous conjugation is defined by a finite exhaustive enumeration; the code computes it for small groups and checks the definition only, not the asymptotic divisibility theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin line 1 is p and s; line 2 is the degree d; line 3 lists permutation generators (0-based image lists, comma separated, semicolon between generators) of a finite group J
# PRECONDITIONS: p prime, s>=0, generators are permutations of range(d); the divisibility theorem needs trivial p-core but the counting itself does not
# OUTPUTS: one line: the total number of conjugation orbits of generating tuples (x,y,u_1..u_n), n=p^s, commutator relation, p-singular u_i, followed by the nonzero per-multiset orbit counts in descending order
# TIME: exhaustive enumeration over p-singular n-tuples and commutator pairs, polynomial in the group order for fixed n
# SPACE: quadratic in the group order for the commutator table
# ARITHMETIC: exact integers only
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and exits nonzero
import sys
from itertools import product

def main():
    lines = sys.stdin.read().splitlines()
    p, s = [int(t) for t in lines[0].split()]
    d = int(lines[1])
    gens = [tuple(int(t) for t in g.split(',')) for g in lines[2].strip().split(';')]
    n = p ** s
    ident = tuple(range(d))
    def mul(a, b):
        return tuple(a[b[i]] for i in range(d))
    def inv(a):
        r = [0] * d
        for i, v in enumerate(a):
            r[v] = i
        return tuple(r)
    def closure(gs):
        seen = {ident}
        fr = [ident]
        while fr:
            nf = []
            for a in fr:
                for g in gs:
                    c = mul(a, g)
                    if c not in seen:
                        seen.add(c)
                        nf.append(c)
            fr = nf
        return seen
    G = sorted(closure(gens))
    order = len(G)
    zc = len([z for z in G if all(mul(z, g) == mul(g, z) for g in G)])
    def eo(a):
        k = 1
        c = a
        while c != ident:
            c = mul(c, a)
            k += 1
        return k
    cid = {}
    for a in G:
        cid[a] = min(mul(mul(g, a), inv(g)) for g in G)
    sing = [a for a in G if eo(a) % p == 0]
    comm = {}
    for x in G:
        for y in G:
            c = mul(mul(x, y), mul(inv(x), inv(y)))
            comm.setdefault(c, []).append((x, y))
    cache = {}
    counts = {}
    for us in product(sing, repeat=n):
        P = ident
        for u in us:
            P = mul(P, u)
        need = inv(P)
        for (x, y) in comm.get(need, []):
            ent = frozenset(us) | {x, y}
            ok = cache.get(ent)
            if ok is None:
                sub = closure(list(ent))
                ok = len(sub) == order
                cache[ent] = ok
            if ok:
                key = tuple(sorted(cid[u] for u in us))
                counts[key] = counts.get(key, 0) + 1
    total = 0
    out = []
    for key, v in counts.items():
        cnt = v * zc // order
        total += cnt
        out.append(cnt)
    out.sort(reverse=True)
    print(' '.join(str(t) for t in [total] + out))

main()
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-finite-groups-only** (formal_scope): All results are for finite groups, with Sylow subgroups, p-singular elements and finite tuple sets taken for granted. Nothing is claimed for infinite or continuous groups. Blocked use: ['Applying the weight equality or the tuple-divisibility count to an infinite or Lie group.', 'Running the tuple enumeration on a group not given by finitely many permutations or generators.']
- **limit-cardinality-only** (formal_scope): Only an equality of cardinalities is proved. The paper builds no canonical or equivariant bijection between irreducible Brauer characters and weights. Blocked use: ['Reading the theorem as a procedure that sends a given simple module to a specific weight.', 'Using the equality to claim a functorial matching of individual characters and weights.']
- **limit-trivial-pcore-hypothesis** (hypothesis): The uniform divisibility count is asserted only for a finite group whose largest normal p-subgroup is trivial. For other groups the paper gives no divisibility guarantee, and the reduction handles them by cancellation of chains. Blocked use: ['Citing a count for a group with a nontrivial normal p-subgroup as evidence for the divisibility theorem.', 'Dropping the trivial p-core check before interpreting divisibility patterns.']
- **limit-tuple-threshold-existential** (nonconstructive): The divisibility theorem asserts only that a threshold exponent exists, depending on the prime, the group and the divisibility exponent. The proof does give a sufficient inequality, but it is a condition on the exponent that makes the number of punctures a very large prime power. It is not a tool for bounding any enumeration. Blocked use: ['Using the threshold as a termination or complexity bound for tuple enumeration.', 'Extrapolating a threshold read off from small-group counts to other groups or primes.']
- **limit-genus-bounded-p-part** (hypothesis): The exact inseparable genus estimate needs a finite extension of the base whose degree has bounded p-part, an exponent large enough for the rounding threshold, and a cover degree bounded by the group order. Outside this regime the index divisibility behind the rounding step is lost. Blocked use: ['Applying the genus bound over an extension whose degree has large p-part.', 'Using the bound with an exponent below the rounding threshold or for covers of unbounded degree.']
- **limit-spherical-base-zorn** (nonconstructive): The spherically complete valued base is obtained as a maximal immediate extension by Zorn's lemma. It is not explicit, and the closure is isomorphic to the complex numbers only as an abstract field. Blocked use: ['Computing in the base field or extracting an algorithm from its construction.', 'Transferring topological or analytic structure through the abstract isomorphism with the complex numbers.']
- **limit-valuation-finiteness-discrete-only** (edge_case): Finiteness of the valuation ring in a finite extension is used only over a complete discrete base. No such finiteness is assumed for an arbitrary nondiscrete valuation ring, which is why connectedness is proved by passing to a Noetherian normal subring. Blocked use: ['Applying coherent finiteness or Stein factorization directly over the nondiscrete valuation ring.', 'Assuming the valuation ring of a finite extension of the spherical base is finite over it.']
- **limit-character-gap-one** (formal_scope): The classification of defect and Brauer counts concerns only blocks whose ordinary count exceeds the Brauer count by one. It classifies numerical data, not blocks up to isomorphism or equivalence. Blocked use: ['Applying the list of admissible pairs to blocks with a different character gap.', 'Inferring the structure or Morita type of a block from its numerical pair.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the numerical blockwise Alperin weight conjecture for every finite group and every prime <i>p</i>. For each <i>p</i>-block <i>B</i>, the number of irreducible Brauer characters in <i>B</i> equals the number of conjugacy classes of <i>B</i>-weights.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The local–global principle in modular representation theory seeks to recover information about representations of a finite group from normalizers of its $p$-subgroups. Alperin’s weight conjecture predicts an exact numerical form of this principle, block by block: simple modules are counted by defect-zero ordinary characters of normalizer quotients. Alperin formulated the conjecture at the 1986 Arcata conference; the original account appeared in the proceedings in 1987 (Alperin 1987; Feng, Li, and Zhang 2023) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Alperin1987](#ref-Alperin1987), [FLZ2023](#ref-FLZ2023)].

Let $p$ be a prime and $G$ a finite group. For an algebraically closed field $k$ of characteristic $p$, a block is a primitive central idempotent of $kG$. For a block $B$, let $l(B)$ be the number of simple $kG$-modules belonging to $B$, equivalently the number of its irreducible Brauer characters. For character calculations we use a splitting $p$-modular system $(K,\mathcal O,k)$: $\mathcal O$ is a complete discrete valuation ring with characteristic-zero fraction field $K$ and residue field $k$, and $K$ splits every subgroup of $G$. A block also denotes its unique central lift to $\mathcal O G$. In [sec:coefficients](#sec:coefficients) we justify reduction to $k=\overline{\mathbb F}_p$ and construct such a system; the resulting counts and block assignments are unchanged over any algebraically closed extension of this field.

For a positive integer $a$, write $a_p$ for its largest power-of-$p$ divisor. A *$p$-weight* of $G$ is a pair $(Q,\phi)$, where $Q\leq G$ is a possibly trivial $p$-subgroup and $$\phi\in\mathop{\mathrm{Irr}}(N_G(Q)/Q),\qquad
 \phi(1)_p=|N_G(Q)/Q|_p.$$ Thus $\phi$ is an ordinary irreducible character of defect zero in the quotient. The block assigned to this weight is defined using the block $b$ of the inflated character in $H=N_G(Q)$.

<a id="eq:block-assignment"></a>
We specify the block-assignment convention. For $H\leq G$, coefficient restriction is the linear map $$s_H:Z(kG)\longrightarrow Z(kH),\qquad
 \sum_{g\in G}a_g g\longmapsto\sum_{h\in H}a_hh.$$ If $c$ is a block of a finite group, its central character $\lambda_c$ is the residue character of the corresponding local factor of the center. In particular it is the scalar action of the center on any simple module in $c$. We say that $b^G=B$ when $$\label{eq:block-assignment}
 \lambda_b\circ s_H=\lambda_B.$$ This is the central-character definition of Brauer block induction (Carlson 1971, 338) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Carlson1971](#ref-Carlson1971)]; see also the corrigendum (Carlson 1973) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Carlson1973](#ref-Carlson1973)]. In the normalizer situations arising from weights, the composite is indeed a central character; we prove this directly in [sec:group](#sec:group). Let $W_p(B)$ be the set of simultaneous $G$-conjugacy classes of weights assigned to $B$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every prime $p$, every finite group $G$, and every $p$-block $B$ of $G$, $$l(B)=|W_p(B)|.$$*

</div>

resolves the numerical blockwise Alperin weight conjecture positively, with no restriction on the defect groups, the prime, or the finite group. The case $Q=1$ is part of the assertion. The conclusion is an equality of cardinalities; a canonical or equivariant correspondence is not required for this numerical statement.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Local–global consequences

The numerical equality also gives the following established consequences of the weight conjecture. Write $\mathop{\mathrm{IBr}}_p(H)$ for the set of irreducible $p$-Brauer characters of a finite group $H$.

<a id="stmt-cor-block-consequences"></a>
<div id="cor:block-consequences" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Local–global and principal-block bounds). *Let $p$ be a prime, $G$ a finite group, $B$ a $p$-block with defect group $D$, and $b$ its Brauer correspondent in $N_G(D)$. Then $$l(B)\geq l(b),\qquad
 D\text{ abelian}\Longrightarrow l(B)=l(b).$$ For $P\in\operatorname{Syl}_p(G)$, one also has $$|\mathop{\mathrm{IBr}}_p(G)|\geq|\mathop{\mathrm{IBr}}_p(N_G(P))|.$$ Let $B_0$ be the principal $p$-block of $G$, and let $k_p(G)$ count the conjugacy classes of $p$-power-order elements, including the identity. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 k_p(G)=3&\Longrightarrow l(B_0)\geq (p-1)/2,\\
 p\mid |G|&\Longrightarrow l(B_0)\geq 2\sqrt{p-1}+1-k_p(G).
 \end{aligned}$$*

</div>

The local block bounds are Alperin’s Consequences 1–2 (Alperin 1987) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Alperin1987](#ref-Alperin1987)]; the principal-block bounds are due to Hung, Sambale, and Tiep (Hung, Sambale, and Tiep 2024, Propositions 3.1–3.2) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[HST2024](#ref-HST2024)]. Their weight-conjecture hypotheses are supplied by [thm:main](#thm:main). The complete derivation, including the passage to the block-free inequality, appears in [sec:consequences](#sec:consequences).

The block-free inequality for $p=2$ was already proved by Malle–Navarro–Tiep (Malle, Navarro, and Tiep 2023, Theorem B) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[MNT2023](#ref-MNT2023)]; the local inequality for maximal-defect $2$-blocks was proved by Feng–Martínez–Rossi (Feng, Martínez, and Rossi 2026, Theorem D) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[FMR2025](#ref-FMR2025)].

The sharper classification of character-count and defect data, and the functorial formulation of the theorem, are given in [sec:consequences](#sec:consequences). We first explain the historical setting and the proof of the local–global equality itself.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Prior work and the proof’s ingredients

Alperin’s formulation extends the notion of weights from the modular representation theory of finite groups of Lie type in defining characteristic to arbitrary finite groups (Alperin and Fong 1990) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[AlperinFong1990](#ref-AlperinFong1990)]. Early large-family cases include Alperin and Fong’s work on symmetric groups and on finite general linear groups in odd modular characteristic (Alperin and Fong 1990) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[AlperinFong1990](#ref-AlperinFong1990)]. Knörr and Robinson expressed the conjecture as alternating sums over chains of $p$-subgroups (Knörr and Robinson 1989) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KnorrRobinson1989](#ref-KnorrRobinson1989)]. Navarro and Tiep reduced the non-blockwise conjecture to conditions on finite simple groups (Navarro and Tiep 2011) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[NavarroTiep2011](#ref-NavarroTiep2011)], and Späth obtained a blockwise reduction (Späth 2013) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Spath2013](#ref-Spath2013)]. These reductions organize the problem around local correspondences for simple groups. Among the resulting advances, Feng, Li, and Zhang proved the blockwise conjecture for finite special linear and special unitary groups and for finite groups with abelian Sylow $3$-subgroups (Feng, Li, and Zhang 2023) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[FLZ2023](#ref-FLZ2023)]. Feng, Malle, and Zhang subsequently studied generic weights for finite reductive groups under explicit hypotheses (Feng, Malle, and Zhang 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[FMZ2026](#ref-FMZ2026)].

Other recent progress includes the inductive blockwise condition for $F_4(q)$ at odd primes not dividing $q$, proved by An, Hiss, and Lübeck (An, Hiss, and Lübeck 2024) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[AnHissLubeck2024](#ref-AnHissLubeck2024)], and Hu and Zhou’s results on inertial blocks, including an alternative proof for $2$-blocks with abelian defect (Hu and Zhou 2025) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[HuZhou2025](#ref-HuZhou2025)]. Huang and Yılmaz develop chain, Galois, and functorial reformulations (Huang and Yılmaz 2026) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[HuangYilmaz2026](#ref-HuangYilmaz2026)]. A recent preprint of Zhang claims the inductive condition for types $\mathsf B$, $\mathsf C$ and sporadic groups (Zhang 2026) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Zhang2026BC](#ref-Zhang2026BC)]; those classes do not exhaust the scope of [thm:main](#thm:main). Our proof instead passes from a direct block-algebra calculation to covers of curves; it does not use a classification of finite simple groups or an inductive weight condition.

The algebraic part uses classical constructions with a specific counting purpose. The subgroup-chain argument is related to the Knörr–Robinson reformulation. Powers modulo commutators belong to Külshammer’s theory of generalized Reynolds ideals (Héthelyi et al. 2005) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[HHKM2005](#ref-HHKM2005)], and conjugation averaging is the group-algebra Higman trace (Higman 1955; Lorenz and Tokoly 2011) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Higman1955](#ref-Higman1955), [LorenzTokoly2011](#ref-LorenzTokoly2011)]. The sum of the $p$-elements detects defect-zero blocks as in Tsushima’s work (Tsushima 1971) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Tsushima1971](#ref-Tsushima1971)]. The passage from central traces to tuples is a weighted genus-one form of Frobenius’s character-counting formula; see (Klug 2025) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Klug2025](#ref-Klug2025)] for the surface-group formulation. We prove the exact versions needed here, including the block projections and normalization of the tuple count. The further assertion is uniform divisibility as the number of punctures increases, not merely a character formula for a fixed surface.

Chen’s relation between Nielsen orbit counts and degrees of moduli of elliptic covers (Chen 2024) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Chen2024](#ref-Chen2024)] informed the geometric divisibility viewpoint. The many-marked, high-index construction and the uniform puncture-divisibility theorem required here are developed in this paper, not supplied by Chen’s theorem.

The geometry combines several established ideas with that divisibility problem. High-index genus-one curves are classical objects in period–index theory; Clark and Lacy prove arbitrary-index existence over infinite fields finitely generated over their prime fields (Clark and Lacy 2019) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[ClarkLacy2019](#ref-ClarkLacy2019)]. Our construction also prescribes marked points giving independent absolute field differentials. The genus calculation belongs to the theory of inseparable genus change initiated by Tate (Tate 1952) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Tate1952](#ref-Tate1952)]; see Schröer (Schröer 2009) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Schroer2009](#ref-Schroer2009)] for a modern treatment. Its height-one determinant identity is a curve-level form of the canonical-bundle formula for $p$-closed foliations (Ekedahl 1988) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Ekedahl1988](#ref-Ekedahl1988)]. Here the calculation must keep additional constants and inseparable residue degrees, and the large index turns a bounded-error estimate into an exact inequality. Finally, maximal-immediate-extension methods of Krull and Kaplansky (Krull 1932; Kaplansky 1942; Poonen 1993) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Krull1932](#ref-Krull1932), [Kaplansky1942](#ref-Kaplansky1942), [Poonen1993](#ref-Poonen1993)] provide a suitable valued field, while the center-valued obstruction studied by Dèbes and Douai (Dèbes and Douai 1997) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[DebesDouai1997](#ref-DebesDouai1997)] explains the arithmetic descent issue for marked covers. We give the required constructions and descent argument directly.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The mechanism of the proof

Chain inversion first reduces the weight equality to an identity between a defect-zero character count and an alternating sum of simple-module counts in subgroup normalizers. A calculation with powers modulo commutators, followed by cancellation of chains, replaces those simple counts by ordinary-character counts. We then construct a central group-algebra element supported on $p$-singular elements, with residue scalars zero or one chosen to remove the defect-zero contribution from the alternating sum. Lift it by class sums to characteristic zero and restrict it to each chain normalizer. The alternating sum of traces of the $p^s$th powers converges to the difference between the alternating ordinary-character sum and the defect-zero count. Expanding the traces gives weighted tuple counts. A further chain cancellation leaves only tuples generating subgroups with no nontrivial normal $p$-subgroup. The complete reduction is [prop:group-reduction](#prop:group-reduction).

Here is the counting problem that remains. Fix a finite group $J$ with $O_p(J)=1$, where $O_p(J)$ is its largest normal $p$-subgroup, and set $n=p^s$. We count ordered tuples $(x,y,u_1,\ldots,u_n)\in J^{n+2}$ satisfying $$[x,y]u_1\cdots u_n=1,\qquad
 \langle x,y,u_1,\ldots,u_n\rangle=J.$$ Here $[x,y]=xyx^{-1}y^{-1}$, and each puncture entry $u_i$ is $p$-singular: its order is divisible by $p$. Prescribe a multiset of $n$ conjugacy classes for the $u_i$, and identify tuples only by simultaneous inner conjugation in $J$, not by permutations of their positions. For every fixed integer $h\geq1$, we must prove that all these counts are divisible by $p^h$ for sufficiently large $s$ (). The threshold may depend on $p,J,h$, but not on the multiset. This uniformity makes each weighted trace sum tend to zero even though the number of multisets grows with $s$.

The geometric construction supplies two properties on the same marked genus-one curve $E/F_0$ in characteristic $p$. First, every divisor degree on $E$ is divisible by $p^s$. Second, after a cyclic splitting extension it becomes a constant elliptic curve with independently varying marked points. More precisely, put $\kappa=\overline{\mathbb F}_p$, choose an ordinary elliptic curve $A_0/\kappa$, and take $F'=\kappa(A_0^n)$. The extension $F'/F_0$ is cyclic Galois of degree $n$ and splits $E$. Over $F'$, the marked points are the generic projection points $t_i\in A_0(F')$. If $\eta$ is a nonzero invariant differential on $A_0$, then the pullbacks $t_i^*\eta$ form a basis of $\Omega_{F'/\kappa}$. Their independence is in this field-differential space, not in the one-dimensional space of regular relative differentials on the elliptic curve. After a finite extension $F/F_0$ with $[F:F_0]_p\leq p^h$, every divisor degree on $E_F$ is still divisible by $p^{s-h}$, and at most $h$ independent differential directions are lost over the compositum $FF'$ ().

For a purely inseparable cover of this curve, a calculation along successive maps of exponent one gives a genus bound with an error controlled by $h$ and the cover degree. The index property makes this bound exact: after multiplication by the cover degree, the difference between the genus term and the ramification expression is a multiple of $p^{s-h}$. The differential calculation bounds that difference below by a negative constant independent of $s$. Once $p^{s-h}$ exceeds the constant’s magnitude, the difference must be nonnegative. This is the rounding step in [thm:inseparable-genus](#thm:inseparable-genus). The resulting estimate is useful independently of the block-algebra reduction: it applies to regular curves over imperfect fields, retains inseparable residue degrees and extra constants, and does not require geometric reducedness.

We lift the marked genus-one curve to mixed characteristic and pass to a spherically complete valued field $K_s$ with value group $\mathbb Q$ and residue field $F_0$. Over an algebraic closure, the generating tuples are precisely the monodromy data of connected $J$-covers of the punctured genus-one curve, with the $J$-action specified. Simultaneous inner conjugacy changes only the chosen fiber point; it does not forget the ordering of the punctures. The lift contains all $|J|$th roots of unity. Galois therefore preserves the local conjugacy classes as it permutes the marked points, so it acts on the finite cover set with the prescribed multiset. This action factors through a finite Galois group $\Gamma$; take a Sylow $p$-subgroup of $\Gamma$.

An orbit of size less than $p^h$ would give a cover defined over an extension $L/K_s$ with $[L:K_s]_p<p^h$. The point requiring proof is that a fixed isomorphism class actually descends with its specified $J$-action. The obstruction is an extension with kernel $Z(J)$. Since $O_p(J)=1$, this kernel has order prime to $p$, and an elementary Sylow argument supplies a descent datum (). Put $F=\operatorname{res}(L)$. The full degree of $L/K_s$ equals $[F:F_0]$, so this residue extension also has small $p$-part.

To rule out such a descended cover $Y$, extend the valuation obtained by reducing functions on the base curve to the function field of $Y$, and take the finite $J$-orbit of one such extension. For each valuation $w$ in this orbit, normalizing $E_F$ in its residue field gives a regular projective curve $C_w$. Reduction of section spaces first shows that the degrees of the maps $C_w\to E_F$ sum to $|J|$. A second comparison imposes vanishing above selected marked points. Together with characteristic-zero Riemann–Hurwitz and the sharp inseparable genus estimate, these section comparisons force $$\chi(Y)=\sum_w\chi(C_w),$$ where each structure-sheaf Euler characteristic is computed over its stated ground field. The equality gives enough sections to construct a finitely presented flat model whose special fiber is the disjoint union of the $C_w$ (). Passing to a Noetherian normal base allows us to apply connectedness. There is therefore only one $C_w$, and its valuation is stabilized by all of $J$. The kernel of the action on its residue field, the inertia group, is consequently normal in $J$. The same genus comparison shows that this kernel is a nontrivial $p$-group, contradicting $O_p(J)=1$.

Every Sylow orbit thus has size at least $p^h$. Its size is a power of $p$, hence is divisible by $p^h$. Summing the orbits proves the uniform tuple divisibility, and [prop:group-reduction](#prop:group-reduction) returns this conclusion to the block identity.

The auxiliary fields in the geometric construction are unrelated to the coefficient system $(K,\mathcal O,k)$ used in the block count.

displays how the two geometric inputs exclude covers over fields whose degree has small $p$-part, and how this returns to the original block count. [sec:foundations](#sec:foundations) records the classical inputs and coefficient choices. [sec:group](#sec:group) proves the algebra-to-counting reduction before any curve is constructed. [sec:twist](#sec:twist) supplies the marked high-index curve, [sec:differentials](#sec:differentials) establishes the sharp genus estimate, and [sec:valuations](#sec:valuations) constructs the valued lift and descends small orbits. [sec:specialization](#sec:specialization) builds the finite model, applies connectedness, and completes both the divisibility theorem and the block identity. Finally, [sec:consequences](#sec:consequences) proves the announced local–global bounds and presents the character-count classification and functorial identity with their full conventions.

<figure id="fig:proof-interfaces">

<figcaption>Mathematical dependencies, not reading order. The covers are geometrically connected $J$-Galois covers, with specified $J$-action over their field of definition, branched only at the marked points and with inertia order divisible by $p$ at every marked point. The two branches provide, respectively, the genus estimate and the arithmetic realization of a hypothetical small Sylow orbit. For sufficiently large $s$, their combination rules out such a cover over a field whose degree has small $p$-part; the geometric cover degree is fixed at $|J|$. The resulting uniform divisibility supplies the counting hypothesis of the block-algebra reduction, which is proved before the geometric construction and applied at the end. No weight-counting theorem is used to construct the curves.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Conventions

All groups are finite. The identity is a $p$-element; a $p$-singular element has order divisible by $p$. We use $[x,y]=xyx^{-1}y^{-1}$ and write $O_p(X)$ for the largest normal $p$-subgroup of $X$. All curve degrees and coherent Euler characteristics are taken over the ground field specified at that point. A regular curve over an imperfect field is not presumed smooth or geometrically reduced. For such a projective curve $C$, we set $$\chi(C)=\chi(\mathcal O_C),\qquad \nu(C)=-2\chi(C).$$ These quantities add on disjoint unions. A valuation is written additively. A field extension is *immediate* if it changes neither the value group nor the residue field.

<a id="sec:foundations"></a>
### Classical foundations used

We state the classical inputs to make the scope of the proof explicit. We also use elementary linear algebra, field and group theory, and the basic definitions and functorial formalism of schemes, sheaves, divisors, and Kähler differentials. The counting, index, inseparable-genus, valued-field, and specialization assertions particular to this proof are established in the later sections. In particular, no weight-counting theorem or conjectural reduction is an input.

<a id="stmt-foundation-groups"></a>
<div id="foundation:groups" class="foundation">

**Foundation 1** (Finite groups). For a finite group $X$, its $p$-subgroups lie in Sylow subgroups of order $|X|_p$. A nontrivial finite $p$-group has nontrivial center and an element of order $p$, and each of its maximal proper subgroups is normal. We use the orbit–stabilizer formula and the usual fixed-point and conjugation actions.

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-algebra"></a>
<div id="foundation:algebra" class="foundation">

**Foundation 2** (Finite-dimensional algebras and characters). The Jacobson radical of a finite-dimensional algebra is nilpotent. Over an algebraically closed field, its semisimple quotient is a product of full matrix algebras, one for each simple module up to isomorphism. A finite-dimensional commutative algebra over an algebraically closed field is a product of local algebras with that field as residue, indexed by its primitive idempotents. Finite group algebras in characteristic zero are semisimple. Over a splitting field the simple endomorphism algebras are the scalar field, and an ordinary irreducible character satisfies $$\sum_{x\in X}\chi(x)\chi(x^{-1})=|X|.$$ Idempotents lift uniquely from the residue algebra of a finite commutative algebra over a complete discrete valuation ring. Nakayama’s lemma applies to finite modules over any local ring.

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-commalg"></a>
<div id="foundation:commalg" class="foundation">

**Foundation 3** (Commutative algebra). A finite-type algebra over a Noetherian ring is Noetherian. Integral extensions satisfy lying over. Closed points of a variety over a field are dense and have finite residue fields over the ground field. For an integral variety, the local dimension at a point plus the transcendence degree of its residue field is the transcendence degree of the function field. A regular local ring is a normal domain; a part of a minimal system of generators of its maximal ideal is a regular sequence and its quotient is regular of the correspondingly smaller dimension. A one-dimensional Noetherian normal local domain is a discrete valuation ring, as is a Noetherian local domain with nonzero principal maximal ideal. Finite torsion-free modules over discrete valuation rings are free. Completion of a discrete valuation ring retains its residue field and uniformizer.

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-fields"></a>
<div id="foundation:fields" class="foundation">

**Foundation 4** (Fields and absolute field differentials). If a finite group $D$ acts faithfully by automorphisms on a field $M$, then $M/M^D$ is Galois of degree $|D|$. Finite separable field extensions are simple. A finite field extension has a maximal separable subextension, and the remaining extension is purely inseparable; the number of embeddings in an algebraic closure is the separable degree. Multiplicative groups of finite fields are cyclic. Algebraically closed fields are classified by their characteristic and transcendence degree. If $S$ is a finitely generated field of transcendence degree $\rho$ over a perfect field $\kappa$, then $$\dim_S\Omega_{S/\kappa}=\rho;
 \qquad [S:S^p]=p^\rho\quad\text{in characteristic }p.$$

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-valuation"></a>
<div id="foundation:valuation" class="foundation">

**Foundation 5** (Valuations over a complete discrete base). A valuation extends to an algebraic field extension. A complete discrete valuation on a field extends uniquely to any finite field extension; its valuation ring is the integral closure of the original ring, is finite over it, and is again a complete discrete valuation ring. The fields to which we apply the latter assertion initially have characteristic zero. No corresponding finiteness assertion for an arbitrary nondiscrete valuation ring is assumed. See (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 00IA) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)] for prolongation and (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 032W, 09EM and 0325) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)] for the complete discrete-base finiteness assertions.

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-descent"></a>
<div id="foundation:descent" class="foundation">

**Foundation 6** (Descent and finite models). A projective scheme with a semilinear descent datum under a finite Galois field extension descends if equipped with a compatible linearized ample line bundle. The same statement holds for a finite *étale* faithfully flat Galois extension of rings and a relatively ample line bundle. Equivariant morphisms and invariant closed subschemes descend along with the scheme. Flatness, smoothness, finiteness, *étaleness*, and relative ampleness in these settings are checked after the faithfully flat extension. Geometric integrality of a finite-type scheme over a field can be checked after an extension of that field. A finite diagram of projective schemes and morphisms of finite presentation over a directed union of fields is defined over a finite stage, including its maps to a base scheme already defined there and the identities among those maps. One may realize projective descent by descent of semilinear modules and graded algebras followed by $\mathop{\mathrm{Proj}}$. For the projective and module descent assertions, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 0CCJ, 0CDR and 0D1V) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. The five locality assertions are respectively (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 02L2, 02VL, 02LA, 02VN and 0D3C) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]; geometric integrality is covered by (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 038K, 0384 and 054P) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. The finite-stage assertion follows from (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 01ZM and 01ZP) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)], including chosen projective embeddings among the finite data.

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-normalization"></a>
<div id="foundation:normalization" class="foundation">

**Foundation 7** (Normalization). The normalization of an integral variety over a field in a finite extension of its function field is finite. The normalization of a finite-type $\mathbb Z$-domain in its fraction field is finite. In the integral closure of a normal domain in a finite Galois extension of its fraction field, the Galois group acts transitively on the primes above any fixed prime of the base. The normalization finiteness assertions follow from the Nagata property in (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0335) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-regularity"></a>
<div id="foundation:regularity" class="foundation">

**Foundation 8** (Regularity, smoothness, and properness). Finite separable scalar extension preserves regularity and reducedness. Smooth varieties over a field are regular. A regular local ring essentially of finite type over a perfect field $\kappa$ is smooth at the corresponding point of a finite-type $\kappa$-model; its module of absolute differentials is free of rank the transcendence degree of the function field over $\kappa$. We use the valuative criterion for properness, including the unique extension of maps from fraction fields of valuation rings, and that proper maps are closed. For regularity and smoothness over a perfect field, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 00TV and 0B8X) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-cohomology"></a>
<div id="foundation:cohomology" class="foundation">

**Foundation 9** (Proper cohomology and ample section rings). Over a Noetherian base, the higher direct images of coherent sheaves under a proper morphism are coherent. Over an affine base their cohomology commutes with flat affine base change. For a projective scheme over an affine Noetherian base and a relatively ample line bundle, sufficiently high powers give projective embeddings, and sufficiently high twists annihilate higher coherent cohomology. The ample section ring is finitely generated and its $\mathop{\mathrm{Proj}}$ recovers the scheme. Finite pullback preserves ampleness; tensor products of relatively ample line bundles are relatively ample, and a line bundle is relatively ample if a positive power is. Formation of $\mathop{\mathrm{Proj}}$ commutes with base change. On a projective curve over a field, coherent cohomology vanishes in degrees greater than one, and for sheaves of finite support in degrees greater than zero. Global functions on a geometrically integral projective variety over a field equal the ground field. For proper coherence, flat cohomology base change, and ample twists, respectively, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 02O5, 02KH and 0B5T) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. For Proj recovery and the ampleness rules, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 0EKI, 0892, 0890 and 02NN) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-duality"></a>
<div id="foundation:duality" class="foundation">

**Foundation 10** (Differentials, adjunction, and duality). We use the transitivity sequence $$\Omega_{V/U}\otimes_VW\longrightarrow\Omega_{W/U}
 \longrightarrow\Omega_{W/V}\longrightarrow0$$ and the conormal sequence for a closed immersion. Let $S$ be a field. On $\mathbb P^b_S$, $\det\Omega_{\mathbb P^b_S/S}=\mathcal O(-b-1)$. For a coherent sheaf $\mathcal G$ there, Serre duality identifies $$H^i(\mathcal G)^\vee
 \simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{b-i}
       (\mathcal G,\mathcal O(-b-1)).$$ We use the local-to-global Ext spectral sequence and the Koszul resolution of a quotient by a regular sequence. These inputs will yield the required absolute determinant-degree formula even when the curve is not smooth over $S$. For the projective-space dualizing complex and the adjunction formalism, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0A9W and Section 48.15) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. The local-to-global Ext sequence is (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 0BQP) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-elliptic"></a>
<div id="foundation:elliptic" class="foundation">

**Foundation 11** (Elliptic curves). A smooth Weierstrass cubic with its point at infinity is an elliptic curve. A generalized Weierstrass equation over a discrete valuation ring with unit discriminant gives a smooth elliptic scheme, polarized by three times its origin. Its relative cotangent line is trivial with invariant differential $\eta$, and multiplication by an integer $a$ pulls $\eta$ back to $a\eta$. For a positive integer $a$, multiplication by $a$ on an elliptic curve or elliptic scheme is finite locally free of degree $a^2$. For the relative group law, see (Deligne and Rapoport 1973, II, Proposition 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[DeligneRapoport1973](#ref-DeligneRapoport1973)]; the unit-discriminant Weierstrass family and the invariant cotangent description are given in (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tags 072S and 047I) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)]. See also (Silverman 2009, vol. 106, chap. III) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Silverman2009](#ref-Silverman2009)] for the Weierstrass formulation. Multiplication is finite flat and surjective by (Milne 2022, Proposition 20.7) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[MilneAV2022](#ref-MilneAV2022)]; over the Noetherian bases here it is finite locally free by (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 02KB) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-complex"></a>
<div id="foundation:complex" class="foundation">

**Foundation 12** (Complex curves and covers). Finite étale algebraic covers of a smooth complex algebraic curve correspond to finite unramified topological covers of its analytification, including morphisms. For a punctured projective curve, normalization of the original projective curve in the resulting algebraic function field gives the finite compact cover. Normal complex curves are smooth, and the local punctured covering completes by a power map, so monodromy cycle lengths give ramification indices. Topological covers are classified by fundamental-group actions on finite fibers. The fundamental group of a genus-one surface punctured at $n$ points has generators $x,y,u_1,\ldots,u_n$ with the single relation $$[x,y]u_1\cdots u_n=1.$$ For a finite map of smooth proper curves over an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero, Riemann–Hurwitz states $$2g(C)-2=\deg(f)\bigl(2g(C')-2\bigr)
              +\sum_{P\in C}(e_P-1).$$ For the algebraic/topological cover correspondence, see (Milne 2008, Theorem 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[MilneLEC2008](#ref-MilneLEC2008)]; for Riemann–Hurwitz, see (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, sec. 53.12, Tag 0C1B) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="stmt-foundation-connectedness"></a>
<div id="foundation:connectedness" class="foundation">

**Foundation 13** (Noetherian connectedness). A proper morphism over a locally Noetherian base whose structure-sheaf direct image is canonically the structure sheaf of the base has geometrically connected fibers. This is the connected-fiber assertion of Stein factorization. We apply it only after constructing a Noetherian normal model, not directly over the nondiscrete valuation ring. See (The Stacks Project Authors 2026, Tag 03H0) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[Stacks](#ref-Stacks)].

</div>

<a id="sec:coefficients"></a>
#### Coefficient fields and scalar extension

We justify the coefficient choice in [sec:introduction](#sec:introduction) without requiring a mixed-characteristic lift of every algebraically closed field. Put $\kappa=\overline{\mathbb F}_p$ and choose an embedding $\kappa\hookrightarrow k$. For any finite group $X$, the ideal $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(\kappa X)\otimes_\kappa k$ is nilpotent, and the quotient of $kX$ by this ideal is the scalar extension of the split matrix product $\kappa X/\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(\kappa X)$. That quotient is again a split semisimple algebra. The ideal is therefore exactly $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(kX)$, and the simple modules correspond under scalar extension.

The center also commutes with this extension: it is the kernel of the finitely many linear maps $a\mapsto ax-xa$, $x\in X$, and field extension preserves kernels. Each local factor of $Z(\kappa X)$ has nilpotent radical and residue $\kappa$; after extension it still has nilpotent radical, now with residue $k$, so remains local. Thus primitive blocks correspond and their simple-module counts agree. Coefficient restriction, restriction to centralizers in the Brauer map, and the quotient maps for normal subgroups all commute with scalar extension. The residue characters of the local central factors extend as well. Consequently the central-character block assignment in <a href="#eq:block-assignment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-assignment">[eq:block-assignment]</a> is preserved, for every subgroup and subquotient involved in a weight. It suffices to prove the numerical identity over $\kappa$.

To construct the coefficient ring, start with $\mathbb Z_p$, lift a tower of finite separable extensions of its residue field $\mathbb F_p$ that exhausts $\kappa$, and complete the union. The construction at the start of the proof of [prop:mixed-lift](#prop:mixed-lift) shows that this gives a complete discrete valuation ring of characteristic zero with residue $\kappa$. In an algebraic closure of its fraction field, choose matrix realizations of every ordinary irreducible representation of every subgroup of $G$. There are only finitely many such representations and matrix entries. Adjoining their entries gives a finite field extension that splits all those subgroups. Its valuation ring is again a complete DVR by [foundation:valuation](#foundation:valuation); its finite residue extension is still $\kappa$, which is algebraically closed. This gives the system $(K,\mathcal O,\kappa)$ used in [sec:group](#sec:group). Quotient representations inflate, so the same coefficients split the required subquotients. These coefficient fields are separate from the auxiliary geometric fields constructed later.

The remaining sections prove the blockwise chain identity, the marked high-index twist, the genus estimate with exact rounding, the required valued-field assertions, and the specialization model. The foundations above supply their classical inputs, not the desired weight equality.

<a id="sec:group"></a>
### From block counts to generating tuples

Fix a prime $p$. Write $O_p(X)$ for the largest normal $p$-subgroup of a finite group $X$, and put $[x,y]=xyx^{-1}y^{-1}$. A $p$-element includes the identity; a $p'$-element has order prime to $p$; and a $p$-singular element has order divisible by $p$. The geometric sections will establish the following precise counting statement. Its uniformity is part of the assertion.

<a id="stmt-thm-tuple-divisibility"></a>
<div id="thm:tuple-divisibility" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Uniform divisibility for generating tuples). *Let $J$ be a finite group with $O_p(J)=1$. For $s\geq0$, $n=p^s$, and a multiset $\mathcal M$ of $n$ conjugacy classes of $p$-singular elements of $J$, let $N_{J,s}(\mathcal M)$ be the number, modulo simultaneous $J$-conjugation, of ordered tuples $$(x,y,u_1,\ldots,u_n)\in J^{n+2}$$ satisfying <a id="eq:tuple-relation"></a>$$\label{eq:tuple-relation}
 [x,y]u_1\cdots u_n=1,\qquad
 \langle x,y,u_1,\ldots,u_n\rangle=J,
 \qquad \{[u_1],\ldots,[u_n]\}=\mathcal M.$$ For every integer $h\geq1$, there is an integer $s_0=s_0(p,J,h)$ such that $$p^h\mid N_{J,s}(\mathcal M)
 \qquad(s\geq s_0)$$ for every such multiset $\mathcal M$.*

</div>

The entries $u_i$ remain ordered: prescribing their multiset of classes does not quotient by permutations. Empty tuple sets have count zero, so the statement includes $J=1$ and groups of order prime to $p$. The proof of [thm:tuple-divisibility](#thm:tuple-divisibility) is completed after [prop:no-small-cover](#prop:no-small-cover). This section proves that it implies the block equality, using [foundation:groups](#foundation:groups), [foundation:algebra](#foundation:algebra) and proving the required counting reductions explicitly.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Coefficient projection and the weight transform

For each ambient finite group $X$, choose a splitting coefficient system $(K,\mathcal O,k)$ as in [sec:introduction](#sec:introduction), [sec:coefficients](#sec:coefficients), with $X$ in place of $G$. Hold this system fixed throughout the calculations with $X$ and its subquotients. This system also splits all subquotients that occur below: a subgroup of a quotient lifts to a subgroup by taking its preimage, and a representation of a quotient inflates to that preimage. These observations apply after any number of subgroup and quotient steps. Ordinary irreducibles may therefore be realized over $K$. Their identification with complex irreducibles, when needed, follows by first splitting the group algebra over $\overline{\mathbb Q}$ and then extending scalars to algebraically closed characteristic-zero fields.

For a central idempotent $E\in kX$, define $$\begin{aligned}
 l(X,E)&=\#\{\text{simple $kX$-modules selected by $E$}\},\\
 k_{\mathrm{ord}}(X,E)&=\#\{\text{ordinary irreducibles selected by the lift of $E$}\},\\
 z(X,E)&=\#\{\chi\text{ selected by that lift}:\chi(1)_p=|X|_p\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here and throughout, modules and characters are counted up to isomorphism. The class sums form a basis of $Z(\mathcal O X)$, and their reductions form a basis of $Z(kX)$. Idempotent lifting over the complete DVR thus gives a unique central idempotent lifting $E$. Every integral central element acts on an ordinary irreducible by a scalar in $\mathcal O$: the scalar lies in $K$ by splitting and satisfies a monic integral equation by finiteness, and $\mathcal O$ is integrally closed. Reduction of this scalar defines a character of $Z(kX)$. The Artinian local decomposition of that center shows that it is precisely $\lambda_b$, where $b$ is the block containing the ordinary irreducible.

For $H\leq X$, let $s_H$ denote coefficient restriction from $Z(kX)$ to $Z(kH)$, or its integral analogue when appropriate. For a $p$-subgroup $Q$, let $\operatorname{Br}_Q$ denote restriction of coefficients from the $Q$-invariants $(kX)^Q$ to $kC_X(Q)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-normalizer-projection"></a>
<div id="lem:normalizer-projection" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:normalizer-projection"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Normalizer projection). *The map $\operatorname{Br}_Q:(kX)^Q\longrightarrow kC_X(Q)$ is a unital algebra homomorphism. Suppose that $$Q\leq H\leq N_X(Q),\qquad C_X(Q)\leq H.$$ Writing $\pi_Q:kH\longrightarrow k[H/Q]$ for the quotient map, one has, for $v\in Z(kX)$, $$\label{eq:normalizer-projection}
 \pi_Q(s_H(v))=\pi_Q(\operatorname{Br}_Q(v)),
 \qquad
 \lambda_b(s_H(v))=\lambda_b(\operatorname{Br}_Q(v))$$ for every block $b$ of $H$. In particular $\lambda_b\circ s_H$ is the central character of a unique block of $X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-normalizer-projection"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-normalizer-projection</summary>

*Proof.* To compute the coefficient of $g\in C_X(Q)$ in a product of two $Q$-invariant elements, let $Q$ conjugate the pairs $(a,b)$ with $ab=g$. Coefficient products are constant on the orbits. Every nontrivial orbit has size divisible by $p$, and the fixed pairs are exactly those with $a,b\in C_X(Q)$. This proves multiplicativity; the identity is preserved.

Each coset $hQ$ is stable under conjugation by $Q$, because $h\in N_X(Q)$. Applying the same orbit cancellation to the sum of coefficients over that coset proves the first identity in <a href="#eq:normalizer-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normalizer-projection">[eq:normalizer-projection]</a>. Also $\operatorname{Br}_Q(v)\in Z(kH)$, since its support lies in $C_X(Q)\leq H$ and it is invariant under $H$.

Every nonzero module for a finite $p$-group over $k$ has nonzero invariants. For completeness, choose a central element $z$ of order $p$; the nonzero kernel of $z-1$, whose $p$th power is zero, is a module for the smaller group $Q/\langle z\rangle$, and induction applies. If the module is simple for $H$, its $Q$-invariants are $H$-stable, and hence the whole module. Thus every simple $kH$-module factors through $H/Q$, proving the second identity. The composition with $\operatorname{Br}_Q$ is a unital algebra character. Characters of the finite commutative algebra $Z(kX)$ are its local residue characters, one for each block, giving uniqueness. ◻

</details>

For $H=N_X(Q)$ put $$\overline E_Q
   =\pi_Q(s_H(E))
   =\pi_Q(\operatorname{Br}_Q(E))\in Z(k[H/Q]).$$ This is an idempotent. Its selected defect-zero quotient characters are exactly the weights assigned to the blocks appearing in $E$. Indeed, the lift of $\operatorname{Br}_Q(E)$ maps to the lift of $\overline E_Q$ by uniqueness of central idempotent lifting. Its action on the inflation of a quotient character is therefore one exactly when the latter is selected by $\overline E_Q$. If $b$ is the block of this inflation, the same test is $$\lambda_b(\operatorname{Br}_Q(E))
   =\lambda_b(s_H(E))=1.$$ By [lem:normalizer-projection](#lem:normalizer-projection), this says that the induced block is one of the blocks in $E$, with the coefficient-projection definition in <a href="#eq:block-assignment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:block-assignment">[eq:block-assignment]</a>. Once $Q$ is fixed up to $X$-conjugacy, there is no further identification of its quotient characters: $N_X(Q)$ acts on $N_X(Q)/Q$ by inner automorphisms.

<a id="eq:weight-transform"></a>
For a conjugation-invariant function $g$ of such pairs $(X,E)$, define $$\label{eq:weight-transform}
 (Tg)(X,E)=
 \sum_{\substack{1<Q\leq X\text{ a $p$-subgroup}\\
                  \text{up to }X\text{-conjugacy}}}
 g\bigl(N_X(Q)/Q,\overline E_Q\bigr).$$ Consequently the desired universal equality for block sums is $l=(1+T)z$. The identity term includes precisely $Q=1$. Every transition in $T$ strictly decreases the $p$-part of the group order, so $T$ is nilpotent on each evaluated pair. Thus the formal inverse $(1+T)^{-1}=\sum_{r\geq0}(-T)^r$ is evaluation-wise finite.

The next step makes the connection with subgroup-chain formulations of the weight conjecture (Knörr and Robinson 1989) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[KnorrRobinson1989](#ref-KnorrRobinson1989)]. We derive the particular normal-chain identity from this finite inverse, retaining its idempotent and orbit data throughout.

<a id="stmt-def-p-chains"></a>
<div id="def:p-chains" class="definition">

**Definition 5** (Chains). A chain in $X$ is a strictly increasing sequence of $p$-subgroups $$\mathcal C=(1=Q_0<Q_1<\cdots<Q_r),
 \qquad |\mathcal C|=r.$$ The length-zero chain $(1)$ is included. Set $$H_{\mathcal C}=\bigcap_{i=0}^rN_X(Q_i),
 \qquad E_{\mathcal C}=\operatorname{Br}_{Q_r}(E).$$ Call $\mathcal C$ *normal* if every $Q_i$ is normal in $Q_r$.

</div>

For every chain, normal or otherwise, $C_X(Q_r)\leq H_{\mathcal C}\leq N_X(Q_r)$, so $E_{\mathcal C}$ is an idempotent of $Z(kH_{\mathcal C})$. For a normal chain one also has $Q_r\leq H_{\mathcal C}$.

<a id="stmt-lem-chain-transform"></a>
<div id="lem:chain-transform" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:chain-inversion"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Finite chain inversion). *The identity $l=(1+T)z$ for all pairs $(X,E)$ is equivalent to $$\label{eq:chain-inversion}
 z(X,E)=
 \sum_{\substack{\mathcal C/X\\\mathcal C\ \mathrm{normal}}}
 (-1)^{|\mathcal C|}l(H_{\mathcal C},E_{\mathcal C}),$$ where $\mathcal C/X$ denotes one representative of each $X$-conjugacy orbit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-chain-transform"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-chain-transform</summary>

*Proof.* We identify the terms of $T^r l$. After $r$ steps they correspond to normal chains of length $r$, with group $H_{\mathcal C}/Q_r$ and idempotent $$\pi_{Q_r}(s_{H_{\mathcal C}}(E))
   =\pi_{Q_r}(E_{\mathcal C}).$$ For the induction, the preimage $Q_{r+1}$ of a nontrivial $p$-subgroup of $H_{\mathcal C}/Q_r$ is a $p$-group strictly containing $Q_r$. It lies in $H_{\mathcal C}$ and therefore normalizes every preceding $Q_i$. Conversely every normal one-step extension arises in this way. Its normalizer in the quotient has preimage $H_{\mathcal C}\cap N_X(Q_{r+1})=H_{\mathcal C'}$. After fixing $\mathcal C$, conjugation is by its stabilizer $H_{\mathcal C}$, which is exactly quotient conjugation at the next step. This proves the orbit assertion without an extra multiplicity.

To check the idempotent at the next step, write $E=\sum_{x\in X}e_xx$ and put $H'=H_{\mathcal C'}$. The new normalizer in $H_{\mathcal C}/Q_r$ is $H'/Q_r$. Restriction to this normalizer retains exactly the coefficient sums over the $Q_r$-cosets contained in $H'$. Choose a set $A$ of representatives for the cosets of $Q_r$ in $Q_{r+1}$. For $h\in H'$, the subsequent quotient has coefficient $$\sum_{a\in A}\ \sum_{q\in Q_r}e_{haq}
     =\sum_{t\in Q_{r+1}}e_{ht}$$ at $hQ_{r+1}$: the sets $aQ_r$, $a\in A$, partition $Q_{r+1}$. These are precisely the coefficients of $\pi_{Q_{r+1}}(s_{H'}(E))$. By [lem:normalizer-projection](#lem:normalizer-projection), this idempotent is $\pi_{Q_{r+1}}(E_{\mathcal C'})$, as required. Every simple $kH_{\mathcal C}$-module factors through $H_{\mathcal C}/Q_r$ and has the indicated idempotent action.
